The fact that, even in these difficult circumstances, Greece still restrains itself in the seventeenth place between thirty-eight states that the public university resists and produces and that despite the methodic "feeling" of the important reform of 2011, its workers, teachers, students, researchers insist on advanceing, overcoming the obstacles that the state itself often places. These islands of progress and excellence are here, they are with us, they invite us to multiply them through a comprehensive reform plan. I will say this again: I have deep faith in the Greek public university, but it will only really open its wings if it is given the opportunity to decide the same thing about his journey to knowledge and research, which this bill is trying to do. It is a proposal for freedom for public universities, a proposal for emancipation, a proposal for freedom, but also for responsibility at the same time. Ladies and gentlemen, we are responding today with a bill which takes three important steps leading to Greek public universities in the new era. Step one: The country acquires a National Higher Education Authority, a body that comes to replace the old ADI, with enhanced responsibilities, but above all, it also constitutes an institutional counterweight to any interventions of the respective government, the Minister in question. It is, in fact, an independent body to promote the autonomy of education in relation to a centralised state-Paterouli. Because, from now on, a national authority will be the state's best archipelago in developing a long-term strategy for higher education, a strategy which - all of us will agree - must go beyond the horizon of the electoral cycle in question. That is precisely why, Minister, it must be made up of high-level, high-level academic persons. The President will be chosen by the Committee on Institutions and Transparency of the Parliament, while the members of the Supreme Council, as well as the Evaluation and Certification Council, will be appointed by the President of the Authority and by two first-degree teachers. Using independently, using international standards, scientifically determined, the members of the new authority will gradually contribute, methodically, to the redeployment, but also to the distribution of parts throughout the territory, and not anymore in terms of localisation or electoral robbery, but in the light of the existing needs and society as well as of the economy. But more centrally, however, the task of this new institution is the continuous evaluation of higher institutions, with criteria that will propose the productive role and the social dimension of universities so that each school can now follow its own dynamics, with measurable objectives constantly being upgraded. It is a process that is bridging with the next large section of the bill, which is no other than the bold step we are taking, to link the evaluation of universities to their funding. Thus, the funds of the state budget will not be made available on the basis of the plans of the Ministers, the interventions of MPs or of various groups of pressure, but in terms of performance in education and in the daily culture that will be formed through schools. The whole of the institutions will be scored through an expanded vendal criteria from their international scientific footprint, from the absorption of their graduates in the labour market, to respect for gender equality, to the provision of accessibility to people with disabilities in their daily operation, as is the case with all the universities in the world. Attention, however, the state continues to guarantee the sustainability of schools. The annual grant will be drawn up by 80% on the basis of objective data - number of students, cost of study, geographical location of each institution. But 20% will be determined by indicators which select the same quality of teaching, originality of scientific research, dynamic, extroversion. All this will be an incentive for greater government funding. The third important step taken by the bill is the liberalisation of research and innovation. These two indicators can today be central to all developed states, as the competitiveness of their economies hides, but in Greece, they are at risk of turning into a nightmare behind four letters: "EUKE", notorious special funds and research bills. They were created as a source of irrigation they were supposed to be the pioneers of scientific programs that were planted in Greek universities. In practice, however, they have come up with more and more dashing, opaque mechanisms, far from their goals. The Minister, like many colleagues, has set out concrete examples of the unbearable bureaucracy in the current framework of the operation of the special accounts, funds and research. That's a change. The new regulations exempt scientific operators from procedural burdens which we feel are completely unnecessary. The work of the PPE is simpler, faster, more transparent at the end, Minister, and less bureaucratic. The targeting of each program will be specified, its evolution will be monitored and its progress will be constantly measurable. This has also been a consistent demand for both researchers and universities throughout the country. We welcome it today. I am sure it will stimulate the innovation and research that the national economy so needs. Ladies and gentlemen, I have briefly presented the three major sections of the bill. It is a follow-up to many initiatives for higher education launched with an effort to restore genuine university asylum. They continued with the launch of foreignlanguage programs and the liberation of postgraduate studies and are accompanied by the intensity of cooperation with renowned institutions from other countries. During our recent visit to the United States, together with the Minister of Education, we had the opportunity to diagnose the greatest interest in American universities and technological institutions in cooperation with Greek universities. For example, in an open invitation that we made to diagnose this interest, to incorporate American universities into a programme for twelve universities to visit our country at the end of March, we had a hundred ninety American universities that showed interest in investigating the prospects for cooperation with Greek public academic institutions. [[Applause from the New Democracy wing]] So you understand the enormous potential that is being opened in this area. I would also like to welcome the important effort made by the National University of Kapodistrian with the first foreign language program offered to students abroad by tuition - in my opinion - extremely reasonable for the quality of the knowledge it will provide and which it has already started to advertise abroad and I can tell you that it is of great interest from foreign students. After all, who would not want to come to study history, philosophy, classical studies in Greece, at the National Kapodistrian University? And yet all these very self-evident things - at least for our own party - have not been carried out for so many years. It is time to break this taboo of ideas and finally make all of us a big leap in the future. [[Applause from the New Democracy wing]] The framework that we are implementing gives academic and research staff of the highest institutions incentives so that they can improve their scientific work and upgrade their own position at the same time. And students in this way will gain access to modern and higher qualifications curriculum programs, which automatically makes their degrees stronger and more competitive. But candidate students will also be selected as the evaluation process will provide them and their families with a clear and objective picture of the level of study of the school or the department they will eventually choose. Eventually, however, universities and this is the most important one, they will have the very opportunity to determine their course of management as well as resources. Of course, Minister, I would like to stress that in this way a burden of responsibility is now passed by the Ministry of Education in the administrations of public universities. Sometimes I feel that there have been manpower and administrations that might have been comfortable with this logic that in the end, the Minister or the Minister of Education should decide on all the important decisions. That changes now. Freedom, as I said before, means responsibility. And this responsibility will be reflected through the evaluation process which will eventually shift the burden of important decisions from the Ministry to the universities themselves. So at least I personally understand the concept of university self-government. Constitutionally guaranteed but, in practice, invalidated by a system that eventually made important decisions always in the Ministry's bureaucracy or ultimately the Minister himself. I would like to stress that the reforms we are talking about today and from which I believe that there will be great benefits for the public university will not cost a single euro, I repeat, not a single euro, to the public funds. Large changes often are not a question of money but of decision. On the contrary, I'd tell you. To be able to turn our country into a regional educational centre by attracting students from abroad, who will pay tuition fees so that they can study undergraduately or postgraduately in Greece, will enable universities to have an important additional source of revenue. The release of EPPs from the bureaucracy of the former regime will enable them to claim more research programmes, to be even more competitive in the European Research Charter. The bill also includes other provisions which I consider to be particularly important, such as the establishment for the first time in the country of a state certificate of informatics, but also the symbolic possibility that we give schools the opportunity to decide the same for school excursions. I remember the Minister telling me how many different stages of permits and signatures they needed to enable a school to make a student trip. That's changing now. We have enough confidence in their schools and addresses to be able to decide for themselves where to go on excursions. [[Applause from the New Democracy wing]] Ladies and gentlemen, the crisis has made us realise that investment in education is a prerequisite for national survival. I believe that we are all leaving behind this fraudulent stereotype of a degree, which is simply the automatic passport for a certain job, either in the public or in the private sector. The times have changed and it is time to adapt to the new data. Today's students are the first to know that the university title they acquire should not be a dry paper. His special weight will be judged by the hard work that took place at the school that provided it. Strict judges of the future will no longer be a public service which will give someone who claims their entry into the public domain rather than an IP. The strict judges of the future will be others. It will be the changing society and the competitive economy. And that's why we don't stop. A comprehensive plan for reforms in secondary and primary education will be followed. We will do so by means of a method, with dialogue without surprises, although we have already described in great detail what our intentions regarding the reform of schools are. But we will hold this dialogue at the same time as the determination that it is time for us to return a page with new lessons, with new teaching methods that will again give teachers, teachers, teachers, managers and not the Ministry. In high school, high school, primary school, kindergarten finally beats the heart of knowledge. And as in pre-school education, in pre-school age, the skeleton of the characters of tomorrow's citizens is shaped in many and possible inequalities that children will accompany for the rest of their lives. Ladies and gentlemen, perhaps in some of my words they sound like advice. But these are my political positions which I express in the language of truth. ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u270	Mr Mamulakis has the floor for seven minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u271	Ladies and gentlemen, today's bill could be entitled 'The three burials of the public and free education of Mrs Kerameos'. With the exception of positive regulations on flexicurity of the PPEs with today's bill, the government and the leadership of the Ministry of Education seek to deprive and downgrade our country's higher education in three distinct ways. Firstly, through linking university funding to their evaluation. We have repeatedly said that SYRIZA also agrees with the evaluation of our university institutions, but the function of evaluation with funding entails great risks for the creation of universities and higher education institutions in general and for two speeds. Secondly, through the equalisation of the professional rights of graduates in private colleges with those of graduates in public universities. And thirdly, through the closure of a large number of new academic departments. With the transformation of the IPP into the New National Higher Education Authority, the Ministry is in fact doing nothing but dismissing responsibility for the establishment, determination of the feasibility and functioning of all academic departments of the country. In this way, a purely political decision, which is what higher education suits the citizens of our country, is transferred to a body of experts under the law which governs the functioning of the new authority, will only be able to give an opinion on the basis of market criteria. However, the main problem with the provisions on the new PPP is the linking of the results of the evaluation, which I mentioned earlier, of the universities carried out by the new authority with the financing of universities, as provided for in Article 16 of this draft law. Besides - what a tragic irony - Article 16 of this bill with Article 16 of the Constitution will differ further. Ladies and gentlemen, the measure of university funding with evaluation criteria comes into effect in specific circumstances that have arisen in the years of the crisis. It will be implemented within a framework of over a decade, which has only partially been tackled, mainly over the last two years. It will be implemented in conditions of extreme sub-station and ageing of our scientific staff. Therefore, the measure of university funding with evaluation criteria will lead with mathematical accuracy to the intensity of disparities between different institutions in the country. The already developed universities, the best staffed and those with dynamic and competitive research infrastructures will draw on more resources. On the other hand, those which have not yet established strong research arms will not be able to obtain this possibility because of their underfinancing and will be led, by manoeuvring, to a vicious sub-financing cycle and, ultimately, to depreciation. Moreover, the equalisation of the professional rights of graduates in colleges with those of graduates in public universities leads to a complete depreciation of the value of the diplomas of the latter. At the same time, the first, after years, major competitions of the AACC that are currently under way are being undermined, as their results will sink into a sea of objections, which will result from the legal undeterminedness that this regulation is causing. Under Article 50, Article 16 of the Constitution, which protects the social good of public higher education, is effectively abolished. At the same time, of course, the performance of full professional rights to the graduates of colleges also operates in addition to the last one in this bill, namely the final closure of thirty-seven - I stress, thirty-seven - under the establishment of new academic departments. Young students, who would be absorbed in the new innovative academic fields created by the previous government and are now artificially abolished, are heading toward the various IEKs, who unfortunately carry the booze of an unexistent English university. Allow me here, however, to refer a little more to the treatment of the newly established Greek Mediterranean University, the founding of which was also passed by the Members of the New Democracy. The establishment of these departments was led by the then Minister of Education, Mr.Gavroglou, following a meeting of a committee made up of academic renowned prestiges, headed by the President of the Foundation for Technology and Research. On this occasion, ELMEPA's founding law was pressed. With regard to these sections, the academic community and the society of Crete have received no response for the reasons why the establishment of the new departments does not meet academic criteria, as the current leadership of the Ministry states. For the departments, which are currently being abolished, there are committees in progress which are responsible for identifying the curriculum and the required infrastructure of the relevant departments. Indicative of the preparation that has been made, for example, in Agios Nikolaos the municipality, in collaboration with the administration of the Hellenic Mediterranean University, have already identified the areas where the sports infrastructure of the now abolished Department of Physical Education and Sport would be developed. These sections would not be included in 2020, but in 2021, and therefore there was plenty of time for their staffing and well-being. In fact, what was targeted was the Greek Mediterranean University's development program for political reasons and, unfortunately, not academics. Indicative of the style and ethos of governance of Mr Digalakis, who also bears his origin from the island, is my corollary, after all, that the administration of the institution was informed - please note this - on the same day that the relevant ministerial decision was adopted. The academic community, in other words, was neither asked nor asked to place itself on the subject, which also illustrates the way in which my fellow State Secretary is aware of the concept of the self-resident of the universities. Ladies and gentlemen, today's law does not honor you or the academic community of the country, which has already expressed a negative attitude to the draft law in question. For this reason, we call on you at the last minute to withdraw the insults of the Constitution Articles 16, 33 and 50. Thank you.

Today's meeting reminds us of situations of the past, when all the educational world reacted and prevented the anti-educational arrangements and circumvention of Article 16 of the Constitution. It would therefore be appropriate to recall this article, which provides that education is a basic task of the state, that all Greeks have the right to free education at all levels, that higher education is provided exclusively by institutions, which are legal entities governed by public law, with full self-government, while those institutions are under the supervision of the State, are entitled to be financially supported by it and the establishment of higher schools by private individuals is prohibited. If we look at Article 16, on points it is clear that this draft law affects provisions of the Constitution. Starting from Articles 1 to 19, we see the abolition of the Quality Assurance and Certification Authority in higher education and its conversion to a National Higher Education Authority. After 15 years of operation of the PPP, the Government shall change the name, the way in which the administration is elected, its responsibilities and intervene with the independent authority by dismantling its administration. It is trying to create a new state-controlled authority and not independently, under the supervision of the Ministry of Education. The most unfortunate thing, however, is that it alters the funding of universities by defining that HEIs will be financed by 80%, on the basis of the general criteria, i.e. the number of students, the size of the university and the cost per student, while the remaining 20% will be provided after an endless hunt of specific criteria defined by the market. More specifically, in order to obtain the rest of the funding, the university will have to achieve some indicators of quality and achievement, research activity, internationalisation, interconnection with the labour market and, if it does not succeed, it will lose that figure. This provision opens a window to reduce the financing of universities, which over ten years have been underfunded, and introduces models of financing applied to the market. No one will be able to guarantee the independence of universities and research work, as they will lead to a search for resources outside the public sector, by way of derogation from the Constitution, while regional universities will be affected much more. By continuing to see that, following the suspension of the two-year vocational training programmes, for PESA graduates and free access departments, the Government is indefinitely suspending the operation of the thirty-seven new PES departments. Let us remember that these departments were proposals from the universities themselves and their abolition circumvents their self-government. The provision mainly affects regional universities. One of these is the Democritus University of Thrace, which, in cooperation with the government of SYRIZA, had tabled concrete proposals for setting up new departments, which were very important to the local society. The current government, however, seems to be deceptively deceptive. I would also like to refer to a very important and topical subject, that of the notice for the appointment of primary and secondary education teachers. Applications that have already begun for primary school teachers deserve to be reminded that state law has been made by the SYRIZA Government. Law 4589/2019 came a year after our country came out of the memos. Our government's aim was to reintroduce one by one to the recruitment to the public, with priority to the recruitment of teachers after eleven years of aptitude. Not only did you vote against this law, but you also mocked it. You have delayed as far as possible the recruitments to special education and within the holidays of Christmas you have received the invitation for teachers of general education, without specifying exactly how many teachers will be recruited by industry. This is the case for minority education teachers IP73 of the minority education programme. Their request is to cover the available organic positions, so that the SYRIZA Government's initiative that included in the planning of minority education is not lost. We therefore call on you to recruit at least those teachers who already work substitutes, who are under fifty in number, as minority education is extremely subordinated and the government needs to show whether or not it is supported. ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u276	Mr. Kouvelas Dimitrios from New Democracy has the floor for seven minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u277	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, this draft law at last lays the foundations for the restructuring and, above all, the modernisation of the operation of higher education in our country, with university institutions autonomous, self-sufficient and, above all, extroversion into practice now and not in words. The university prepares tomorrow's professionals in each sector and it is therefore absolutely essential that they be on a parallel and common footing with the labour market. Thus, the university should closely monitor developments and be a pioneer in both theory and research. The fourth industrial revolution was almost complete. Our university should at least be associated with her. The first and necessary step achieved by this draft law is the substantial strengthening of the independent authority, the PPP so far, which ensures the quality of higher education, through the establishment of the new National Higher Education Authority. There are three main axes to this bill, as presented in detail by the Ministry of Education's leadership in the work of the committee responsible. The first axis concerns the National Higher Education Authority established with specific objectives. First of all, the existing powers with regard to certification are expanded, the principle is strengthened with new, individual responsibilities, its members are now selected by additional guarantees and, furthermore, there are two important and important councils, which will assist the work of the authority. It shall be the Board and the Evaluation and Certification Board. The establishment of the new principle constitutes strengthening, protecting and, of course, upgrading our higher education. The University in Greece is now commonplace that in recent years it has been deceived. The education of young people has been dealt with with with horrific and ideological syndromes of other times. It also operated as a bunker sanctuary and asylum for illegal activities. Now, once the asylum regime has been upgraded, one of the first and very important reforms of the Mitsotakis Government, we give the university the distinctive place it deserves in a favoured and organised state. The second axis of the bill relates to the liberalisation of the PPE accounts, i.e. the Research Fund Special Accounts, which is the central tool for research funding, carried out at universities and research and technology bodies. The new legislative framework seeks to avoid the bureaucratic turbulence of the existing framework, which has so stumbled in this case at universities, and to achieve greater flexibility in the functioning of these specific accounts while increasing accountability and transparency. So more research programmes will be put in place and we will thus reverse the trend towards the underreporting of available European funds. Finally, under the former regime research at our universities was led to underfunding. This will prove to be in the interest of new researchers who are thirsty for incentives to stay in our homeland rather than undone abroad for better luck. On the other hand, we cut the distribution of money to universities. To date this has been done some as every minister wanted. It could, in other words, decide so much, much smaller than the available funds, to go to more or less, even to a university institution. That's a change. Strict and fair criteria are being introduced. Regular university funding of 80% will now be allocated on the basis of objective criteria relating to the number of students, the type of the respective curriculum and the geographical location of each institution. In particular, the latter will be an incentive to attract and valueworthy external researchers and collaborators to the "current" programmes of universities in the border regions and our islands. Recently we discussed the Democritus University in Thrace where ideas and appetites exist many of the teachers there. However, it is difficult to attract - and this is what we were told - remarkable external partners because of the very low remuneration resulting from the limited funding provided by the research programmes. Therefore, objective criteria for 80% of the regular funding and for the remaining 20%, this will be linked to quality criteria, such as, for example, the size of the research activity developed by a specific institution, how many research funds have already been used in previous years, how many Greek students send for training programmes abroad, how many foreign students finally attract to the Greek university, as well as what kind and especially the number of partnerships they conclude with relevant foreign educational institutions. Even what are the possibilities of accessing small and medium-sized enterprises in these universities. In other words, evaluation indicators will also act as incentives for upgrading the university and procedures. The third axis concerns other arrangements aimed at resolving operational school problems from school to school support. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to refer to a very sensitive and delicate issue, which is not one of the many teachers. I am pleased that, as a new Member of Parliament from the beginning of this governance, I can see that there is very much confrontation with both the Minister and the responsible Secretary-General in order to meet the legitimate demands of these fellow citizens. With this draft law, we are paying particular attention to supporting the multitude of family. Multitude teachers will now be able to join special transfer categories and so, as a priority, move from both region to region and within the region itself. I would therefore suggest that we all bend together on the issue of the appointment of multitude teachers and, above all, on the review of the criteria and molecules given to them. The molecules cannot be just three for each child, for every minor child. We must see this again, ladies and gentlemen. The times we have spent, ladies and gentlemen, are demanding and the new societal challenges are great. One thing is certain: the main pillar of progress for Greece was and it will always be its education. And since we talk about education, we always come to mind talk of great ancient Greeks. So Diogenes said that education for young people is sound, for poor riches and for rich ornament. Let us not forget that in the end, ladies and gentlemen, everything is a matter of education. I too support the vote in favour of this bill. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u278	Mr. Ioannis Pascalides from New Democracy has the floor for seven minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u279	Thank you, Mr President. Ministers, the innovative regulation of this draft law, which provides for the financing of educational institutions to be carried out through evaluation, is the great change in the field of education, which even represents an overall change of attitude, since, to date, each Minister has been able to decide on the basis of the financing of HEIs. Thus, with the new bill, powers are being dismantled from the Ministry of Education to universities, and objective and qualitative funding criteria are laid down. More specifically, in Chapter I of this Bill, Articles 1-19, the new principle of higher education under the title of the National Authority of Higher Education, the objective of which is to reward the effort, as to date there have been no objective criteria and criteria of merit. The National Higher Education Authority will replace the limited and pre-existing Quality Assurance and Certification Authority in Higher Education. It will be composed of the President, who will be chosen by the Ministerial Council, following an open public notice to Greek universities in Greece and abroad, the Supreme Council, the Evaluation Board and the Director-General, while the specific responsibilities will be the National Strategy Programme for Higher Education and the allocation of the annual grant budget at the IITs. The most important task of the national authority through its Board of Governors, of course, will be to allocate 20% of this funding through evaluation, which will in fact be the incentive to the HEIs to improve on objectives that they choose, within the framework of their autonomy, for example in the quality of education, research activity, outward-looking actions, while the remaining 80% of the regular grant will be allocated to HEIs according to the total number of students registered, the estimated cost of study per student, the geographical spread of the Foundation, etc. In particular, the Assessment and Certification Council will be responsible for the evaluation of EITs, the fulfilment of the quality requirements for organising curricula I, II and III cycles, the certification of internal quality assurance systems for EITs and curricula, as well as for carrying out thematic evaluations of EITs, in particular as regards the internationalisation strategy, gender equality, access for disabled people, extroversion, etc. If an IIT fails to achieve the quality indicators to which it has chosen to be assessed, then the grant it will not receive as a consequence of its assessment will be allocated as a grant for the other IITs. It is also worth noting that the institution of excellence comes back and under Article 19 of this draft law, the emergence of centres of excellence in higher education is institutionalised. Criteria for the promotion of centres are the excellent quality and effectiveness of teaching and research work, the linking of teaching and research, etc. In Chapter B of this Legislation, Articles 20-27, an important part of the provisions of the Eighth Chapter of Law 4485/2017 is certified and a more flexible and adaptable framework is established for specific research funds accounts, which are the central tool for financing research carried out at universities and research and technology bodies. Thus, the new legislative framework seeks to avoid the bureaucratic obstacles to the existing framework and achieve greater flexibility and functioning of the PPECs, while at the same time increasing accountability and transparency, while brake the loss of research resources. Chapter III of the Bill, Articles 28-32, starts with Article 28, which introduces the State Certificate of Informatics for the first time in our country, which is obtained by exams and will certify the knowledge of computer handling in the processing of computer sheets and Internet services, while allowing for the extension, by decision of the Ministry of Education and Religious Affairs, of certification to other digital objects. In Chapter D, Articles 33-58, and in particular Article 33, the start of the thirty-seven new departments established by the previous Government is suspended, since their evaluation procedure has not been complied with by the competent bodies. Compliance with this procedure will make it possible to assess the new parts by the new authority, on the basis of which they are linked to the market. In order to attract students from abroad, Article 43 of the bill makes it easier to attract foreign students to learn Greek. In addition, as the Minister said, the aim of the Ministry is to strengthen initiatives for the extroversion of Greek HEIs, such as summer curricula, where students will combine studies with holidays, as well as synergies between Greek and foreign HEIs, expressing the appreciation that by 2024 some forty to fifty thousand foreign students will participate in English-speaking courses of classical literature, philosophy and ancient history. It is, indeed, very important that Greece is finally planning to make use of its cultural and historical wealth to attract Chinese and other foreign students to universities, as part of the broader reform of higher education. It is notable to regulate Article 55, where, in view of the protection of the multitude of families, the multitude of teachers now fall into specific transfer categories so that they may be transferred from region to region as a priority, but within the same region. To sum up, I would like to mention that this bill is the beginning so that the Greek universities have acquired the extroversion that had been lost in recent years as institutions, and have the opportunity to obtain more funding instruments beyond the state budget, giving, for example, incentives for donations, private-public-public partnerships and patent revenues. We want the public education system to be strengthened, and that is why the 2020 budget has been strengthened by EUR 113 million. We want the internationalization of our universities. We want our country to become a higher education centre in south-eastern Europe, and we are sure that we will achieve this, since the new institutional framework not only sets out criteria for establishing sections on the basis of a comprehensive strategic planning, but initiatives such as the liberalisation of study programmes and the establishment of foreign-language departments are being strengthened. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2020-01-21-S1-commons.u281	Mr President, Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, the government with this bill restores public higher education to the level that it deserves and is indeed the level that all Greek citizens are claiming to send their children to the public university. And yet, our universities have remarkable teachers, with international publications, an important research project that competes with the largest foreign universities. Our students differ wherever they are found and are sought on the international labour market. I am one of them, and I must say that the University of Athens gave me all the facilities I needed to meet the international competitive environment, without any delay to the graduates of the renowned centres. So the question is why, unfortunately, all this is not reflected in the international indicators or in the image of the Greek universities. In order to do this, it obviously needs a strategic plan and an assessment, no matter how panic some are hearing that word. There must be a strategic plan, because the objects taught must introduce young people into the labour market, not for now but for ten years later. In planning today’s education we must see the future, those branches that will emerge in the next decade. It needs to be evaluated precisely for adapting to the increasingly evolving needs and markets. Both these axes exist in this draft law with the establishment of the National Higher Education Authority of the NISA with increased independence, enhanced responsibilities in relation to the ADI and the main objective of a long-term strategic project in the field of higher education through a restructuring of the academic map on the basis of objective and qualitative criteria and not of political purposes, the conclusion of programming agreements with universities enabling them to develop their own strategic design and link the annual grant to objective criteria but also to rate indicators, but also adapted to the character and potential points of each university. The opposition stands at this point to criticise the bill. But logic here is not punishable. The aim is to give an extra incentive and reward to universities to escape the stagnation that is the enemy of progress, to innovate and evolve. I cannot, of course, as an academic, raise the question of the underfunding of the Greek universities or even of the remaining funding in relation to the fixed needs over time. I think that our next major objective should be the greatest investment in higher education, coupled with moves to strengthen the quality of the education project - which are already being carried out - will give the country a real impetus as a whole. At the same time, our institutions must also be able to claim more money not only from state resources but also through forms of cooperation with the private sector, donations and PPPs. All this must always be in addition to the existing budget. That must therefore be made clear. Do we want universities that produce citizens of the world and scientists with the highest level of knowledge and training, or universities that will make armies of unemployed people, who will be looking for Members' political offices for a job? That is the main question. Particularly important are the arrangements for the release of the PPE, this tight bureaucratic framework of public accounting. The clergy of course fall into the scientific person, who loses his role and essentially by an academic researcher is converted into an accountant, even for costs such as graphics. This is a reality and the nightmare that we must live every time when we want to manage the EPP's funds and funds today. Restrictive arrangements stifle creativity, discourage research effort and ultimately lead to the loss of money, which could be exploited for the benefit of universities as a whole, and in many cases are based on the specific accounts for modernising their infrastructures and covering significant costs. It is of course understandable that the PPE should be excluded from the public accountancy, where it entered in the light of greater transparency. With this bill, the Ministry of Education proceeds to a series of areas and structural reforms, such as the National Authority of Higher Education with enhanced responsibilities, the liberalisation of research through the creation of a new framework for ΕΚΕ, the introduction of a state certificate of informatics, the strategic policy on the quality upgrading of higher education, the safeguarding of the administration of the International University of Greece, the strengthening of the attraction of foreign students, the rehabilitation and recognition of professional rights in accordance with European Union law, the care for the multiple family and the safeguarding of the uninterrupted functioning of our schools. The above are some of the most important points in this bill as they restore injustices, deal with arrhythmias and ensure those prospects needed for a modern and effective educational system. In a long process of dialogue with all involved actors and civil society a live consultation brought together all the views and assessments of the parties involved and a bill was finally drafted that covers basic obligations. After all, as we have stressed many times, this government, the Government of the New Democracy and Mr Mitsotakis, has the priority of its education and extroversion. Replacement of the IPP by the HRC is the first step. Enhancing the responsibilities and role of the national authority in relation to the Quality Assurance Authority we confirm the determination to complete and substantial upgrade our universities. We are introducing a number of innovations, such as the provision of funding for institutions also depends on their evaluation, which clearly leads to the continuing support and upgrading of schools. We certify the curriculum before they begin and not during the course. We examine the possibilities and conditions for sections I, II and III cycles to be operational and attempt the self-assessment of research centres. The programme budget thus becomes self-evident, distinct from the other projects of the research centre. We stop the logic of division budgets per year for multi-annual programmes and draw up global budgets, while budget reforming is now carried out quarterly without the burden of researchers. At the same time, we are simplifying the procedures and the bureaucracy of undertaking from the investigator so that we can really put the burden on research and not on the researchers' administrative work. Another important point in the current bill is the introduction of the state certificate of informatics. This is the first time this has happened in our country. The certification of digital skills is a key necessity in the labour market today. Ladies and gentlemen, education - I think we all agree on this - can no longer be the subject of micro-political purposes. It is certainly a place that favours consensus and cooperation as it is about our future, about the future of our children, with the opportunities for progress and advancement in this place. I am sure that we can make the start today by supporting and voting in favour of this bill. Thank you very much.

That is the question we are having to answer today and we are being asked to answer not only as parliamentarians but also as parents who are struggling for the future of our children, who are seeing the nightmare of unemployment knocking at their door, while at the same time another door opens that of the outside, intensifying a phenomenon that took place in the years of the crisis in our country huge proportions. I invite you to consider not only your narrow party interest, but see what is going on around us. All the countries of the developed world at the time of the fourth industrial revolution that we are living in have found certain ways of dealing with the issue I raised, that is, those changes in their universities that are related to the labour market while investing in research in the areas that have a comparative advantage. They have thus succeeded in dramatically reducing their main structural unemployment. This is obviously not the case in our country. Attention! I'm not saying we don't have good teachers or good students. On the contrary, yes. I say something different, that the strategic orientation of our country's university studies, whether at the TEI or at the TEI, has become distorted, with customer anti-developmental logic. SYRIZA's previous government just understood. How could he, anyway? Sticked in her obsessions, against all forms of evaluation and excellence and against the opening up of higher education with the revision of Article 16. I only give one example of the negative consequences of this logic. The Minister, Mr Dygalakis, said so at the last meeting of the committee. We all know about the fourth industrial revolution that changes the way we work and also the way we think about it. Artificial intelligence and data science are its main priorities. Do you know, ladies and gentlemen, how many of the sixty study programmes established in the last year of SYRIZA governance are they dealing with the subject of data science? Zero! None! Unbelievable and true, at the moment, in fact, that all over the world, programs of study in data science are constantly being established. So we could not continue with this strategic planning, colleagues of the Major Representation. With this bill we are attempting to put higher education in the orbit of modern times. We upgrade for this purpose the IPP to a National Higher Education Authority. We are creating objective criteria for checking the quality of the sections, schools or universities being established. This national authority for the first time will recommend a national education strategy, related to a more general national development strategy in our country and in our economy. It will create objective criteria for evaluating all departments, schools and universities, of which 80% will in fact depend on their funding. It also puts the innovative logic of the incentive into the remaining 20%, with criteria for growth and harmonisation with the European environment. What are these criteria? Firstly, absorption in the labour market. Secondly, the quality of the educational staff and the services that this offers students. Thirdly, the safeguarding of professional rights. Fourthly, the institution's extroversion, i.e. its cooperation with relevant universities in other European countries or major universities in non-European Union countries. In Chapter 2, also important, the bill simplifies the procedures of the EPPC and lays rules for transparency and efficiency of the ongoing research in Greece, trying to help universities recover the lost space on the research and technology market. It is not enough, of course, to reply from the heavy public accounting system, as the majority of the university community has been calling for for so many years. Because the funds managed by the PPE are public. And it is, in my opinion, another reason why universities should seek additional resources from the market. This is what would make them more competitive on the basis of healthy competition between them, therefore with those of other countries, which is also one of the objectives of the bill. Because I see, Minister, that you are also making arrangements for the PPE of the Open University, I would like to say to you that the payroll of the partner training staff is dramatically delaying and I would like you to make sure that there are no many months' delays as they existed in the past. With regard to the remaining provisions, the Ministry makes areas such as the State Certificate of Informatics in our schools, which is more than necessary at the time of the information we live. I shall conclude by returning to major opposition. Think of your vote again today. Stop giving back fights to the country's education, especially to the highest. The debate on colleges has already proved to be a pretext. You know very well where higher education and research are going in the advanced countries of Europe and the rest of the world. You have shown a person of consensus on the issue of the election of the President of the Republic. Show real consensus and substantial discussion with proposals on an issue that really is the key to the armoring of the country's economy, the upgrading of higher education and research in our country. We are all voting for this bill. Thank you.
